things to handle for the volunteer group, I’ll do the first part, like printing the maps, but then I may forget to call the museum desk or leave the folders half-ready until someone reminds me.\n- key: S006\n  name: Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance\n  description: Often avoids, dislikes, or is reluctant to engage in tasks that require sustained mental effort.\n  manifestation: If I know I need to read through a long exhibit description or plan out a talk, I keep finding little chores around the house to do first because sitting with all that reading feels like pulling teeth.\n- key: S007\n  name: Losing_Items\n  description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I’m always putting down the camera battery, name tags, or the envelope with sign-up sheets before I was twelve.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: The museum group has had to quietly shift some planning pieces away from me, and I’ve stopped feeling confident leading the art walks the way I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: It’s been noticeably causing trouble for about two years and four months, since my friend died and I took over the museum liaison duties by myself.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 63 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S003\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s probably nothing too serious, but I’ve noticed I’m having a little trouble when people are talking to me. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, trying to listen, but then I realize I missed the middle part of what they said. I end up asking them to run it by me again. I just thought maybe I should check whether that’s… you know, something worth looking into.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble hearing people when they talk to you, or is it more that you just can't seem to follow what they're saying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s my hearing, really. It’s more like… I’ll be looking right at them, like when someone at the museum is telling me about the next art walk, and I’m trying to listen, but somehow I miss the middle of what they said. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again. I mean, I might forget little things here and there, like anyone, but nothing recent stands out to me as a serious issue. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following instructions or completing tasks?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not in a way that I mean to ignore things. If someone gives me a few things to handle for the volunteer group, I’ll usually do the first part, like printing the maps, and then I might forget the next piece, like calling the museum desk. Or I’ll leave the folders kind of half-ready until someone reminds me. It’s not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions causing any problems for you at work or in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: A little bit, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but with the museum group, I think they’ve started quietly giving some of the planning parts to other people instead of me. Nobody’s been unkind about it, but I notice it.\n\nAnd I’ve… I guess I don’t feel as confident leading the art walks as I used to. I worry I’ll miss something or not keep things straight. Does that count as a problem in daily activities?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Does that count as a problem in daily activities?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family due to these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any big way. I hope that’s answering what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or depressed about these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say depressed, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down because of these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve been feeling sad or down because of it. Maybe a little disappointed in myself sometimes, but not really sad, if that makes sense. I try to just correct it and move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions happening consistently over time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It hasn’t been forever, exactly, but it’s been pretty noticeable for… maybe about two years and a few months now.\n\nIt seemed to start causing more trouble after my friend died and I ended up taking over the museum liaison duties by myself. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but since then I’ve had to pay more attention to instructions and details on my own, and that’s when it became more obvious. Does that answer what you mean by consistently over time?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s probably nothing too serious, but I’ve noticed I’m having a little trouble when people are talking to me. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, trying to listen, but then I realize I missed the middle part of what they said. I end up asking them to run it by me again. I just thought maybe I should check whether that’s… you know, something worth looking into.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble hearing people when they talk to you, or is it more that you just can't seem to follow what they're saying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s my hearing, really. It’s more like… I’ll be looking right at them, like when someone at the museum is telling me about the next art walk, and I’m trying to listen, but somehow I miss the middle of what they said. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again. It’s not all the time, I don’t think, but enough that I noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're having trouble paying attention to what's being said, or do you think you're just not understanding it when you do hear it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more paying attention than understanding. I mean, if I catch what they say, it makes sense. It’s just that someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, trying to listen, and then I realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble with paying attention happen every day or just sometimes?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s every day, exactly… or at least I don’t notice it every day. It’s more just sometimes, like at the museum when someone’s talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to go over it again. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but it’s happened enough that I wondered if I should mention it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed a big problem with that. I mean, I might forget little things here and there, like anyone, but nothing recent stands out to me as a serious issue. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following instructions or completing tasks?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not in a way that I mean to ignore things. If someone gives me a few things to handle for the volunteer group, I’ll usually do the first part, like printing the maps, and then I might forget the next piece, like calling the museum desk. Or I’ll leave the folders kind of half-ready until someone reminds me. It’s not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions causing any problems for you at work or in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: A little bit, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but with the museum group, I think they’ve started quietly giving some of the planning parts to other people instead of me. Nobody’s been unkind about it, but I notice it.\n\nAnd I’ve… I guess I don’t feel as confident leading the art walks as I used to. I worry I’ll miss something or not keep things straight. Does that count as a problem in daily activities?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family due to these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any big way. My family and friends haven’t really said anything to me about it, at least not that I can think of.\n\nMaybe there are small moments where someone has to remind me of something, but I wouldn’t say it’s changed the relationship. Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t call it a huge formal change. With the museum group, some of the planning parts have kind of been shifted away from me quietly. Nobody made a big announcement or anything, and honestly it may be better that way, but I do notice it.

It’s more like… I’ll be looking right at them, like when someone at the museum is telling me about the next art walk, and I’m trying to listen, but somehow I miss the middle of what they said. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again. It’s not all the time, I don’t think, but enough that I noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're having trouble paying attention to what's being said, or do you think you're just not understanding it when you do hear it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more paying attention than understanding. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but it’s happened enough that I wondered if I should mention it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed a big problem with that. I mean, I might forget little things here and there, like anyone, but nothing recent stands out to me as a serious issue. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following instructions or completing tasks?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not in a way that I mean to ignore things. If someone gives me a few things to handle for the volunteer group, I’ll usually do the first part, like printing the maps, and then I might forget the next piece, like calling the museum desk. Or I’ll leave the folders kind of half-ready until someone reminds me. It’s not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions causing any problems for you at work or in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: A little bit, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but with the museum group, I think they’ve started quietly giving some of the planning parts to other people instead of me. Nobody’s been unkind about it, but I notice it.\n\nAnd I’ve… I guess I don’t feel as confident leading the art walks as I used to. I worry I’ll miss something or not keep things straight. Does that count as a problem in daily activities?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family due to these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any big way. My family and friends haven’t really said anything to me about it, at least not that I can think of.\n\nMaybe there are small moments where someone has to remind me of something, but I wouldn’t say it’s changed the relationship. I hope that’s answering what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or depressed about these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say depressed, no. Maybe a little worried or embarrassed when I realize I missed a step, but I try not to dwell on it too much. It’s more like, “Oh, I should’ve done better with that,” and then I hope nobody thinks badly of me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down because of these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve been feeling sad or down because of it. Maybe a little disappointed in myself sometimes, but not really sad, if that makes sense. I try to just correct it and move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions happening consistently over time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I wouldn’t want to say I can’t manage day to day, because I still do. I still try, but I’m more hesitant now. So yes, it is affecting things, just… maybe not in a dramatic way. So I guess it has changed things some, just not in a dramatic way. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your trouble with paying attention and following instructions has affected your friendships or relationships with family members?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not that I’ve noticed in a clear way. My family hasn’t said anything, and I don’t think friendships have changed much because of it.\n\nI suppose I might be a little more careful or apologetic sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to blame that on attention issues unless someone else noticed it too. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but it’s happened enough that I wondered if I should mention it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed a big problem with that. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really speech, I don’t think—I can talk okay. It’s more like… sometimes when people at the museum are talking to me about the next art walk, I’m looking right at them and trying to listen, and then I realize I missed the middle part of what they said. It’s more just sometimes, like at the museum when someone’s talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to go over it again. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but it’s happened enough that I wondered if I should mention it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed a big problem with that. I mean, I might forget little things here and there, like anyone, but nothing recent stands out to me as a serious issue. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following instructions or completing tasks?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not in a way that I mean to ignore things. If someone gives me a few things to handle for the volunteer group, I’ll usually do the first part, like printing the maps, and then I might forget the next piece, like calling the museum desk. Or I’ll leave the folders kind of half-ready until someone reminds me. It’s not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions causing any problems for you at work or in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: A little bit, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but with the museum group, I think they’ve started quietly giving some of the planning parts to other people instead of me. Nobody’s been unkind about it, but I notice it.\n\nAnd I’ve… I guess I don’t feel as confident leading the art walks as I used to. I worry I’ll miss something or not keep things straight. I try to just correct it and move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with paying attention and following instructions happening consistently over time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I wouldn’t want to say I can’t manage day to day, because I still do. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
